Optimal Eigenvalue Rigidity of Random Regular Graphs
Horng-Tzer Yau

Abstract
Consider the normalized adjacency matrices of random d d -regular graphs on N N vertices with fixed degree d ⩾ 3 d\geqslant 3 , and denote the eigenvalues as λ 1 = d / d − 1 ⩾ λ 2 ⩾ λ 3 ⋯ ⩾ λ N \lambda_{1}=d/\sqrt{d-1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\lambda_{3}\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{N} . We prove that the optimal (up to an extra N o N ⁡ ( 1 ) N^{\oo_{N}(1)} factor, where o N ⁡ ( 1 ) \oo_{N}(1) can be arbitrarily small) eigenvalue rigidity holds. More precisely, denote γ i \gamma_{i} as the classical location of the i i -th eigenvalue under the Kesten-Mckay law in decreasing order. Then with probability 1 − N − 1 + o N ⁡ ( 1 ) 1-N^{-1+\oo_{N}(1)} , | λ i − γ i | ⩽ N o N ⁡ ( 1 ) N 2 / 3 ​ ( min ⁡ { i , N − i + 1 } ) 1 / 3 , for all ​ i ∈ { 2 , 3 , ⋯ , N } . \displaystyle|\lambda_{i}-\gamma_{i}|\leqslant\frac{N^{\oo_{N}(1)}}{N^{2/3}(\min\{i,N-i+1\})^{1/3}},\quad\text{ for all }i\in\{2,3,\cdots,N\}. In particular, the fluctuations of extreme eigenvalues are bounded by N − 2 / 3 + o N ( 1 ) N^{-2/3+\oo_{N}(1)} . This gives the same order of fluctuation as for the eigenvalues of matrices from the Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble.



University of Pennsylvania

huangjy@wharton.upenn.edu

Stanford University

theom@stanford.edu

Harvard University

htyau@math.harvard.edu

1
Introduction

A random
d
-regular graph on
N
vertices is chosen uniformly from the set of all
N
-vertex
d
-regular graphs. These graphs serve as ubiquitous models in various fields such as computer science, number theory, and statistical physics. Particularly, they find applications in constructing optimally expanding networks
[
35
]
, analyzing graph
\zeta
-functions
[
57
]
, and studying quantum chaos
[
56
]
.

A fundamental problem in the study of random regular graphs is the analysis of the spectrum of the adjacency matrix, or equivalently, the normalized adjacency matrix denoted as
H

H\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{A}{\sqrt{d-1}}.

(1.1)

Normalizing in this manner facilitates comparisons across different values of
d
and with other matrix ensembles. The eigenvalues of
H
are denoted as
\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{N}
. According to the Perron-Frobenius theorem, since all vertices have degree
d
,
\lambda_{1}=d/\sqrt{d-1}
is always the largest eigenvalue in modulus. The second largest eigenvalue is particularly significant as it determines the spectral gap.

The Alon-Boppana bound
[
53
]
asserts that the second largest eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}
of
any
family of
d
-regular graphs with growing diameter satisfies
\lambda_{2}\geqslant 2-\oo_{N}(1)
, where
2
is the spectral radius of the adjacency operator on the infinite tree; see
[
41
]
. Alon in
[
1
]
conjectured that this bound is tight for random regular graphs, proposing that
\lambda_{2}\leqslant 2+o_{N}(1)
with high probability. This conjecture was proven in
[
28
]
by Friedman, and later an alternative proof was given by Bordenave
[
18
]
. Their proofs are based on the moment methods, involving classifying walks of different lengths and cycle types, and the error term
in eigenvalue is of order
\OO((\log\log N/\log N)^{2})
.
In
[
40
]
, the first and third authors of the present paper, building upon joint work
[
12
]
with Bauerschmidt, presented a new proof of Alon’s conjecture with a polynomially small error of
\OO(N^{-c})
(for some small
c>0
). This proof is based on a careful analysis of the Green’s function. Moreover, this approach also establishes the concentration of all eigenvalues, not just
\lambda_{2}
.

The precise location of the second eigenvalue is a crucial problem with applications in computer science and number theory
[
35
]
. Graphs for which
\max\{\lambda_{2},|\lambda_{N}|\}\leqslant 2
are known as
Ramanujan graphs
, introduced by Lubotzky, Phillips, and Sarnak in
[
55
]
. They are the best possible spectral expander graphs. A major open question in combinatorics and spectral graph theory is whether there exist infinite families of Ramanujan graphs for every
d\geqslant 3
. Algebraic constructions exist when
d-1
is a prime power
[
46
,
49
,
52
]
. Moreover, there are infinite families of
bipartite
Ramanujan graphs, for which
\lambda_{1}=-\lambda_{N}=d/\sqrt{d-1}
, and
\lambda_{2},|\lambda_{N-1}|\leqslant 2
for any
n
and
d
, constructed through interlacing families of the expected characteristic polynomial
[
47
,
48
]
.
It was conjectured based on numerical simulations that the distribution of the second largest eigenvalue of random
d
-regular graphs after normalizing by
N^{2/3}
is the
same as that of the largest eigenvalue of the Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble
[
51
,
55
]
. Namely, there is a constant
c_{N,d}
so that
N^{2/3}(\lambda_{2}-2)-c_{N,d}
has the Tracy-Widom distribution
[
59
]
, and similar statement holds for the smallest eigenvalue.
This would imply that the fluctuations of extreme eigenvalues are of order
\OO(N^{-2/3})
if
c_{N,d}
is of order one.
If
c_{N,d}=0
(which seems to be the most probable scenario), then it would imply that slightly more than half of all
d
-regular graphs are Ramanujan graphs.

In this work, we take a major step towards this universality conjecture. We derive an optimal bound (up to an extra
N^{\oo(1)}
factor) on the fluctuations of extreme eigenvalues, i.e. the fluctuations are bounded by
N^{-2/3+\oo(1)}
.

The empirical eigenvalue density of random
d
-regular graphs converges to that of the infinite
d
-regular tree, which is known as the Kesten-McKay distribution; see
[
41
,
50
]
. This density is given by

\varrho_{d}(x):=\mathbf{1}_{x\in[-2,2]}\left(1+\frac{1}{d-1}-\frac{x^{2}}{d}\right)^{-1}\frac{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}{2\pi}.

For
2\leqslant i\leqslant N
, we expect the
i
-th largest eigenvalue of the normalized adjacency matrix
H
to be closed to the classical eigenvalue locations
\gamma_{i}
, where
\gamma_{i}
satisfies

\int_{\gamma_{i}}^{2}\varrho_{d}(x){\rm d}x=\frac{i-1/2}{N-1},\quad 2\leqslant i\leqslant N.

(1.2)

Our main results given optimal concentration for each eigenvalues of the random
d
-regular graphs.

Theorem 1.1
.

Fix
d\geqslant 3
. There is a positive integer
\omega_{d}\geqslant 1
depending only on
d
, such that with probability
1-N^{(1-\oo(1))\omega_{d}}
, the eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i\in[\![{N}]\!]}
of the normalized adjacency matrix of a
d
-regular random regular graph satisfy

\displaystyle|\lambda_{i}-\gamma_{i}|\leqslant N^{-2/3+\oo(1)}(\min\{i,N-i+1\})^{-1/3}

(1.3)

for every
2\leqslant i\leqslant N
and
\gamma_{i}
are the classical eigenvalue locations, as defined in (
1.2
).

The aforementioned theorem implies that
\lambda_{2},|\lambda_{N}|=2+N^{-2/3+\oo(1)}
. In the bulk, for fixed
\delta>0
and
\delta N\leqslant i\leqslant(1-\delta)N
, the fluctuation of
\lambda_{i}
is of order
N^{-1+\oo(1)}
. This level of fluctuation aligns with that observed for the eigenvalues of matrices from the Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble, and more broadly, Wigner matrices; see
[
22
]
. While our discussion thus far has centered on
\lambda_{2}
, it’s worth noting that the spectral statistics of every eigenvalue are of interest from a statistical physics perspective, and have been subjects of study since the early works of Wigner and Dyson
[
60
,
21
]
.

We prove
Theorem

1.1
through a careful analysis of the Green’s function and their variations, which has proven a highly successful way to analyze spectral information of random matrices (see
[
24
]
for an overview of this approach). In the rest of this section, we introduce the notations and state our main result on the optimal estimates of the Green’s function.

We define the
Green’s function
of the normalized adjacency matrix
H
by

G(z)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(H-z)^{-1}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{{\bm{u}}_{i}{\bm{u}}_{i}^{\top}}{\lambda_{i}-z},\quad z\in\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\vcentcolon\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]>0\}.

We denote the normalized trace of
G
, which is also the Stieltjes transform of the empirical eigenvalue density of
H
, by

m(z)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i}G_{ii}(z)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}-z}.

(1.4)

We refer to
m(z)
simply as the
Stieltjes transform
.
The
Ward identity
states that the Green’s function
G
satisfies

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j}|G_{ij}(z)|^{2}=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[G_{ii}(z)]}{N\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]},\qquad\frac{1}{N}\sum_{ij}|G_{ij}(z)|^{2}=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m(z)]}{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]}.

(1.5)

Our goal is to approximate
m(z)
by
m_{d}(z)
,
the Stieltjes transform of the Kesten–McKay law

\displaystyle m_{d}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\varrho_{d}(x){\rm d}x}{x-z},\quad z\in\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\vcentcolon\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]>0\}.

We recall the semi-circle distribution
\varrho_{\rm sc}(x)
, its Stieltjes transform
m_{\rm sc}(z)
, and the quadratic equation satisfied by
m_{\rm sc}(z)
,

\displaystyle\varrho_{\rm sc}(x)=\bm{1}_{x\in[-2,2]}\frac{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}{2\pi},\quad m_{\rm sc}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\varrho_{\rm sc}(x){\rm d}x}{x-z}=\frac{-z+\sqrt{z^{2}-4}}{2},\quad m_{\rm sc}(z)^{2}+zm_{\rm sc}(z)+1=0.

Explicitly the Stieltjes transform of the Kesten–McKay law
m_{d}(z)
can be expressed in terms of the Stieltjes transform
m_{\rm sc}(z)
,

\displaystyle m_{d}(z)=\frac{1}{-z-\frac{d}{d-1}m_{\rm sc}(z)}.

(1.6)

The following Theorem states that
m(z)
can be approximated by
m_{d}(z)

for
z
belonging to the following spectral domain

\mathbf{D}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\vcentcolon|E|\leqslant 2+{\mathfrak{a}},0<\eta\leqslant 1/{\mathfrak{a}},N\eta\sqrt{\min\{|E-2|,|E+2|\}+\eta}\geqslant N^{{\mathfrak{a}}}\},

(1.7)

where
0<{\mathfrak{a}}<1
can be arbitrarily small. Theorem
1.1
is a consequence of the following theorem.

Theorem 1.2
.

For every
d\geqslant 3
, there is a positive integer
\omega_{d}\geqslant 1
such that for sufficiently small
0<{\mathfrak{a}}<1
, and
N
large enough, with probability
1-\OO(N^{-(1-\oo(1))\omega_{d}})
, the following is true for every
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbf{D}
(recall from (
1.7
)),

\displaystyle|m(z)-m_{d}(z)|\leqslant N^{\oo(1)}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{N\eta},&-2\leqslant E\leqslant 2,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}}\left(\frac{1}{N\eta^{1/2}}+\frac{1}{(N\eta)^{2}}\right),&|E|\geqslant 2,\end{array}\right.

where
\kappa=\min\{|E-2|,|E+2|\}
.

Theorem
1.2
provides optimal concentration of the Stieltjes transform of the emprical eigenvalue distribution of
H
. The imaginary part of the Stieltjes transform has long been utilized as a means to access information about the empirical eigenvalue density, and precisely following such reasoning leads to Theorem
1.1
. We present the proof of
Theorem

1.1
using
Theorem

1.2
as an input to
Section

3.4
.

1.1
Proof ideas

To establish Theorem
1.2
, we start with the self-consistent equation of a modified Green’s function quantity, as introduced in
[
12
,
40
]
. The quantity is the average of
G_{jj}^{(i)}(z)
over all pairs of adjacent vertices
i\sim j
:

\displaystyle Q(z;{\mathcal{G}}):=\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{i\sim j}G_{jj}^{(i)}(z).

(1.10)

For
d
-regular graphs, although intricate,
Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})
emerges as a more useful entity than the Stieltjes transform of the empirical eigenvalue distribution of the normalized adjacent matrix
H
.
To compute
G_{jj}^{(i)}(z)
, we approximate it by the Green’s function of a neighborhood of radius
\ell
around vertex
j
, with vertex
i
removed, incorporating suitable weights at each boundary vertex. Given that most vertices in random
d
-regular graphs possess large tree neighborhoods, the majority of vertices
j
in the summation of (
1.10
) have large tree neighborhoods. For such vertices
j
, we can subsequently replace the boundary weights in the approximation with the weight
Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})
. Furthermore, the neighborhoods of these vertices
j
, with vertex
i
removed, are truncated
(d-1)
-ary trees of depth
\ell
.

Let
Y_{\ell}(Q(z;{\mathcal{G}}))
be the Green’s function at the root vertex of a truncated
(d-1)
-ary tree of depth
\ell
, with boundary weights
Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})
. This leads us to the following self-consistent equation for
Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})
:

\displaystyle Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})=\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{i\sim j}G_{jj}^{(i)}(z)\approx Y_{\ell}(Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})).

(1.11)

for arbitrary
\ell
. The fixed point of the above self-consistent equation (
1.11
) is given by the Stieltjes transform of the semi-circle distribution
m_{\rm sc}(z)
. Indeed,
Y_{\ell}(m_{\rm sc}(z))
represents the Green’s function at the root vertex of an infinite
(d-1)
-ary tree, whose spectral density is governed by the semi-circle distribution. Consequently, the self-consistent equation (
1.11
) was utilized in
[
12
,
40
]
to demonstrate that
Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})
is closely approximated by
m_{\rm sc}(z)
with high probability:

\displaystyle m_{\rm sc}(z)=Y_{\ell}(m_{\rm sc}(z)),\quad Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})\approx m_{\rm sc}(z).

The Stieljes transform
m(z)
of the empirical eigenvalue distribution of
H
can also be recovered from the quantity
Q
, through the following approximation

\displaystyle m(z)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{ii}(z)\approx X_{\ell}(Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})),

(1.12)

where
X_{\ell}(Q(z;{\mathcal{G}}))
denotes the Green’s function at the root vertex of a truncated
d
-regular tree of depth
\ell
, with boundary weights
Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})
.

Utilizing the self-consistent equations (
1.11
) and (
1.12
),
[
12
,
40
]
established that with high probability, uniformly for any
z
in the upper half-plane with
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\geqslant N^{-1+\oo(1)}
, the Stieltjes transform of the empirical eigenvalue distribution closely approximates
m_{d}(z)
:

\displaystyle|m(z)-m_{d}(z)|\leqslant N^{-\delta},

(1.13)

for some small
\delta>0
. Furthermore, it was shown that the eigenvectors are completely delocalized. Achieving optimal rigidity of eigenvalue locations, as stated in Theorem
1.1
, necessitates an optimal error bound much stronger than
N^{-\delta}
in (
1.13
). This constitutes a standard problem in random matrix theory, which requires an optimal error bound for the high moments of the self-consistent equation:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})-Y_{\ell}(Q(z;{\mathcal{G}}))|^{2p}],

(1.14)

for large integers
p>0
. Analogous estimates have been established for Wigner matrices
[
25
,
26
,
33
]
, Erdős-Rényi graphs
[
43
,
23
,
22
,
32
,
37
,
38
,
42
]
,
d
-regular graphs with growing degrees
[
11
,
39
,
31
]
, and
\beta
-ensembles
[
20
]
.

Distinguished from Wigner matrices, the primary challenge in studying the adjacency matrices of random
d
-regular graphs lies in the correlations between matrix entries, where row and column sums are fixed at
d
. To address this constraint, the concept of local resampling has been pivotal, initially explored to unravel randomness under such correlations. This technique was first employed to derive spectral statistics for random
d
-regular graphs with
d=N^{\oo(1)}
in
[
13
]
, and subsequently extended in
[
12
,
40
]
to establish the self-consistent equation (
1.11
) and bound its high moments (
1.14
). In this method, local resampling randomizes the boundary of a neighborhood
{\mathcal{T}}
(as opposed to randomizing edges near a vertex as in
[
13
]
) by exchanging the edge boundary of
{\mathcal{T}}
with randomly chosen edges elsewhere in the graph. Notably, this local resampling is reversible, meaning the law governing the graphs and their switched counterparts is exchangeable.

However, the error bound for the high moments of the self-consistent equation (
1.14
) in
[
40
]
falls short of optimality, as do the results regarding eigenvalue rigidity.
Our main contributions extend these findings to the optimal scale. Specifically, we demonstrate that fluctuations in extreme eigenvalues are bounded by
N^{-2/3+\oo(1)}
. This improves the weak
N^{-\oo(1)}
bound in
[
40
]
, and gives the same order of fluctuation as for the eigenvalues of matrices from the Gaussian orthogonal ensemble.
Below, we outline the challenges encountered, and the new strategy and key ideas developed to overcome them.

1.1.1
New Strategy

To illustrate the ideas that will lead to an optimal error bound for (
1.14
), let’s begin with the first moment of the self-consistent equation:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})-Y_{\ell}(Q(z;{\mathcal{G}}))]=\mathbb{E}[G_{oo}^{(i)}-Y_{\ell}(Q(z;{\mathcal{G}}))]=\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}-Y_{\ell}(Q(z;{\mathcal{G}}))],

(1.15)

where in the first statement we exploit the permutation invariance of vertices, so the expectation of
Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})
is the same as the expectation of
G_{oo}^{(i)}
for any pair of adjacent vertices
i\sim o
. Given that most vertices have large tree neighborhoods, we can focus on cases where
o
has a large tree neighborhood. The second statement arises from the local resampling process. Instead of computing the expectation of
G_{oo}^{(i)}
, we perform a local resampling around vertex
o
, by switching the boundary edges of the radius
\ell
neighborhood
{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
of vertex
o
, with randomly selected edges
\{(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
from
{\mathcal{G}}
.
We denote the resulting graph as
{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}
, its Green’s function as
\widetilde{G}(z)
, and the new boundary vertices of
{\mathcal{T}}
after local resampling as
\{c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
, which are typically distanced apart in terms of graph distance (see Section
2.2
for further details). As local resampling is reversible,
G_{oo}^{(i)}
and
\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}
share the same law and expectation, which gives the last expression in (
1.15
).

To demonstrate the smallness of the final expression in (
1.15
), we expand
\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}
using the Schur complement formula.
The radius
\ell
neighborhood of
o
in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}^{(i)}
(where vertex
i
is removed) is
{\mathcal{T}}^{(i)}
, a truncated
(d-1)
-ary tree at level
\ell
.
The Schur complement formula states that
\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}
is the same as the Green’s function of
{\mathcal{T}}^{(i)}
with boundary weights given by
\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(\mathbb{T})}
, which are the Green’s functions of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}^{(\mathbb{T})}
(with the vertex set
\mathbb{T}
of
{\mathcal{T}}
removed). With high probability, the new boundary vertices
\{c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
are far from each other, and exhibiting large tree neighborhoods. Consequently, the neighborhoods of
c_{\alpha}
in
{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}
are given by the truncated
(d-1)
-ary trees, and the boundary weights can be approximated by the Green’s function of
{\mathcal{G}}
(the graph before switching) as

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(\mathbb{T})}\approx G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}\approx\bm{1}({\alpha=\beta})Q(z;{\mathcal{G}}),\quad 1\leqslant\alpha,\beta\leqslant\mu.

(1.16)

Consequently, the leading-order term of

\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}
is given by
Y_{\ell}(Q(z;{\mathcal{G}}))
, and (
1.15
) is small. This strategy, utilized in
[
40
]
, provided a weak bound for (
1.14
), by bounding all errors from the approximations such as (
1.16
) by
N^{-\delta}
for some small
\delta>0
.

To achieve optimal estimates for Green’s functions, we need to analyze the approximation errors from the Schur complement formula (
1.16
) more carefully. These errors comprise weighted sums of terms involving factors such as:

\displaystyle(G^{(b_{\alpha})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}-Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})),\quad G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})},\quad Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})-m_{\rm sc}(z).

(1.17)

For a more precise description of these error terms, we refer to
Lemma

4.1
. Instead of simply bounding each term in (
1.17
) by
N^{-\delta}
, we carefully examine all possibilities of error terms.
The crucial observation is that the expectation of the first factor in (
1.17
) with respect to the randomness of the simple switching is very small:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{S}}[G^{(b_{\alpha})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}-Q(z;\mathcal{G})]=\OO\left(\frac{1}{N^{1-\oo(1)}}\right);

and, up to negligible error, the expectation of the second factor in (
1.17
) can be expressed to include the first factor,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bf S}[G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\alpha}b_{\beta})}]=\frac{d}{\sqrt{d-1}}\mathbb{E}_{\bf S}\left[(G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}-Q(z;{\mathcal{G}}))G_{b_{\alpha}c_{\beta}}^{(b_{\beta})}\right]+\text{``negligible error"}.

In this way, we demonstrate that either the error term is negligible or contains one “diagonal factor” akin to the first factor in (
1.17
). Such a factor,
(G^{(b_{\alpha})}_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}-Q(z;{\mathcal{G}}))
, is in the same form as the expression (
1.15
) we initiated with. To evaluate the expectation, we will perform another local resampling around the vertex
c_{\alpha}
, chosen randomly from the last local switching step.

Our new strategy to derive the optimal error bound for (
1.14
) is an iteration scheme. At each step, we perform local resampling and rewrite the Green’s function of the switched graph in terms of the original graph. Next, we show that each term contains at least one “diagonal factor”
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}
where
(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})
is an edge selected during local resampling, or it is negligible. Then, we can perform a local resampling around
c_{\alpha}
, and repeat this procedure. We formalize this iterative scheme using a sequence of forests, as introduced in
Section

3.2
. Crucially, we demonstrate that each iteration of local resampling yields an additional factor of at least
N^{-\delta}
for some small
\delta>0
. After a finite number of steps, all error terms become negligible, leading to an optimal bound for the high moments of the self-consistent equation (
1.14
). This iteration is detailed in
Proposition

3.9
and
Proposition

3.10
.

1.1.2
New Technical Ideas

For each local resampling, we need to rewrite the Green’s function of the switched graph in terms of the original graph. While the Schur complement formula suffices for expressions like (
1.15
), for higher moments, we must also track changes such as:

\displaystyle(\widetilde{Q}-Y_{\ell}(\widetilde{Q})-(Q-Y_{\ell}(Q))=(1-Y_{\ell}^{\prime}(Q))(\widetilde{Q}-Q)+\OO(|\widetilde{Q}-Q|^{2}),

where
\widetilde{Q}=Q(z;{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
and
Q=Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})
. Since
\widetilde{H}-H
(the difference of the normalized adjacency matrices of
{\mathcal{G}}
and
{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}
) is low rank, one can use the Ward identity (
1.21
) to show that with high probability

\displaystyle|\widetilde{Q}-Q|\leqslant\frac{N^{\oo(1)}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m(z)]}{N\eta},\quad\eta=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z],

as done in prior work
[
40
]
. However it is insufficient for the optimal rigidity.

To achieve improved accuracy for
\widetilde{Q}-Q
, it is essential to compute the difference of the Green’s functions
\widetilde{G}(z)
and
G(z)
for the graphs
{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}
. The local resampling around vertex
o
can be represented by a matrix
\xi:=\widetilde{H}-H
. Thus using the resolvent identity, we can write

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}(z)-G(z)=-G(z)\xi G(z)-\sum_{k\geqslant 1}G(z)\xi(-G(z)\xi)^{k}G(z).

(1.18)

The aforementioned expansion was utilized in
[
39
]
to prove the edge universality of random
d
-regular graphs, when the degree
d=N^{\oo(1)}
grows with the size the graph. In this case, owing to our normalization, each entry of
\xi
scales as
\OO(1/\sqrt{d})
. Thus, the terms in (
1.18
) exhibit exponential decay in
1/\sqrt{d}
. However, in our scenario where
d
is fixed, this decay is too slow.

For
d\geqslant 3
with
d
fixed, instead of relying on the resolvent expansion (
1.18
), we introduce a novel new expansion based on the Woodbury formula, taking advantage of the local tree structure as detailed in
Lemma

4.3
. Specifically, since the rank of the matrix
\xi=\widetilde{H}-H
is at most
\OO((d-1)^{\ell})
(the number of edges involved in local resampling), let’s denote the eigenvalue decomposition of
\xi
as
\xi=UCU^{\top}
. The Woodbury formula yields the the difference of the Green’s functions
\widetilde{G}(z)-G(z)
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}(z)-G(z)

\displaystyle=-G(z)U(C^{-1}+U^{\top}G(z)U)^{-1}U^{\top}G(z).

(1.19)

Here, the nonzero rows of
U
correspond to the vertices involved in local resampling. Therefore, the term
U^{\top}G(z)U
in (
1.19
) depends solely on the Green’s function entries restricted to the subgraph
{\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{B}}_{\ell+1}(o,{\mathcal{G}})\cup\{(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
. With high probability, these randomly chosen edges
(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})
have tree neighborhoods, and are far apart from each other and the vertex
o
. Let
P(z)
denote the Green’s function of the tree extension of
{\mathcal{F}}
(extending each connected component to an infinite
d
-regular tree). Then
U^{\top}G(z)U-U^{\top}P(z)U
is small, leading to the following expansion

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}(z)-G(z)=G(z)F(z)G(z)+\sum_{k\geqslant 1}G(z)F(z)((G(z)-P(z))F(z))^{k}G(z).

(1.20)

Here,
F(z)=-U(C^{-1}+U^{\top}P(z)U)^{-1}U^{\top}
is an explicit matrix. When restricted to the subgraph, each entry of
G(z)-P(z)
is smaller than
N^{-\delta}
for some small
\delta>0
. Hence, the terms in (
1.20
) exhibit exponential decay in
N^{-\delta}
which is much faster than (
1.18
). And we can truncate the expansion (
1.20
) at some finite
k
, and the remainder is negligible.

Another technical idea involves a Ward identity-type bound for the entries of the Green’s function, which serves to constrain various error terms. The Ward identity plays a crucial role in mean-field random matrix theory. It states that the average over the Green’s function entries can be controlled by the Stieltjes transform of the empirical eigenvalue distribution, thus ensuring smallness:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{ij}|G_{ij}(z)|^{2}=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m(z)]}{N\eta},\quad\eta=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z].

(1.21)

Consequently, when selecting two vertices randomly from our graph, the Green’s function entries are expected to have small modulus. While some error terms adhere to this form, we also encounter Green’s function entries taking such as

G_{ij}^{(o)}
,
where
i,j
are two adjacent vertices of a vertex
o
, i.e.
o\sim i,o\sim j
. Namely, we take two vertices of distance two, delete their common neighbor, then take the Green’s function. In the initial graph
{\mathcal{G}}
,
i
and
j
have distance two, so
G_{ij}
is not small.
In
Proposition

5.1
, we establish a Ward identity type result for the expectation of
|G_{ij}^{(o)}|^{2}
, similarly to (
1.21
). The proof again leverages the idea of local resampling. By local resampling around vertex
o
, we reduce the computation to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|G_{ij}^{(o)}(z)|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[|\widetilde{G}_{ij}^{(o)}(z)|^{2}],

(1.22)

for the switched graph. We then expand it using the Schur complement formula, similarly to (
1.15
). Crucially, we can bound (
1.22
), by itself times a small factor, and errors as in (
1.21
), leading to the desired bound given by the right-hand side of (
1.21
).

In summary, the iteration scheme presented in this paper for the computation of the high moments of self-consistent equation (
1.14
) offers a potent method
to analyze the spectral properties of random
d
-regular graphs. This method yields optimal rigidity for the eigenvalues of random
d
-regular graphs (up to an additional
N^{\oo(1)}
factor).
Random
d
-regular graphs can also be constructed from
d
copies of random perfect matchings, or random lifts of a base graph containing two vertices and
d
edges between them. This class of random graphs obtained from random lifts and in particular their extremal eigenvalues have been extensively studied

[
6
,
7
,
27
,
17
,
29
,
54
,
45
,
19
]
. It would be interesting to explore if the approach in this paper can be applied to analyze extremal eigenvalues in this setting.
Moreover, our results establish a
N^{-2/3+\oo(1)}
fluctuation bound for the second-largest and the smallest eigenvalue, matching that of the Gaussian orthogonal ensemble. We hope this can be utilized in the future to prove the edge universality of the extremal eigenvalues for random
d
-regular graphs.

1.2
Related Work

The eigenvalue statistics of random graphs have been intensively studied in the past decade. Thanks to a series of papers
[
10
,
36
,
43
,
23
,
22
,
32
,
37
,
38
,
42
,
11
,
39
,
31
]
, the bulk and edge statistics of Erdős–Rényi graphs
G(N,p)
with
Np\geqslant N^{\oo(1)}
and random
d
-regular graphs with
d\geqslant N^{\oo(1)}
are now well understood. Universality holds; namely, after proper normalization and shifts, they agree with those from Gaussian orthogonal ensemble. For random
d
-regular graphs, we anticipate such a universality phenomenon holds even for a fixed degree
d\geqslant 3
. However the situation is dramatically different for very sparse Erdős–Rényi graphs.

In the sparser regime
Np=\OO(\ln N)
, for Erdős–Rényi graphs, there exists a critical value
b_{*}=1/(\ln 4-1)
such that if
Np\geqslant b_{*}\ln N/N
, the extreme eigenvalues of the normalized adjacency matrix converge to
\pm 2

[
14
,
3
,
58
,
15
]
, and all the eigenvectors are delocalized
[
4
,
23
]
. For
(\ln\ln N)^{4}\ll Np<b_{*}\ln N/N
, there exist outlier eigenvalues
[
58
,
3
]
.
The spectrum splits into three phases: a delocalized phase
in the bulk, a fully localized phase near the spectral edge, and a semilocalized phase in between
[
5
,
2
]
. Moreover, the joint fluctuations of the eigenvalues near the spectral edges form a Poisson point process. For constant degree Erdös-Rényi graphs, it was proven in
[
34
]
that the largest eigenvalues are determined by small neighborhoods around vertices of close to maximal degree and the corresponding eigenvectors are localized.

This paper focuses on the eigenvalue statistics. The eigenvectors of random
d
-regular graphs are also important. The complete delocalization of eigenvectors in
\ell_{\infty}
norm was proven in previous works
[
12
,
40
]
. For sparse random regular graphs, several results provide information on the structure of eigenvectors without relying on a local law. For example, random regular graphs are quantum ergodic

[
8
]
, their local eigenvector statistics converge to those of multivariate Gaussians
[
9
]
, and their high-energy eigenvalues have many nodal domains
[
30
]
. Gaussian statistics have been conjectured in broad generality for chaotic systems in both the manifold and graph setting
[
16
]
. A rich line of research exists toward this idea. For an overview in the manifold setting, see the book
[
61
]
, and for the graph setting, refer to
[
56
]
.

As mentioned above, the second eigenvalue governs the spectral gap of the matrix. Showing the expansion properties of random regular graphs and attempting to find deterministic families of graphs that exhibit these same properties has been a major area of research. For example, the simple random walk on random regular graphs is known to have cutoff
[
44
]
, and these graphs are optimal vertex expanders (see
[
35
]
).

1.3
Outline of the Paper

In
Section

2
, we recall the concept of local resampling and present results on the estimation of the Green’s function of random
d
-regular graphs from
[
40
]
. In
Section

3
, we state our main results,
Theorem

3.2
, regarding the high moments estimate of the self-consistent equation, and prove both
Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.2
, utilizing
Theorem

3.2
as input. We also outline an iteration scheme to prove
Theorem

3.2
. For this iteration scheme, we will repeatedly express the Green’s functions after local resampling in terms of the original Green’s functions before local resampling. In
Section

4
, we gather estimates on the difference in Green’s functions before and after local resampling. Additionally, in
Section

5
, we collect bounds, which will be used to constrain various error terms encountered in the iteration scheme. Finally, the proof of
Theorem

3.2
is detailed in
Section

6
.

1.4
Notation

We reserve letters in mathfrak mode, e.g.
{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{b}},{\mathfrak{c}}
, to represent small universal constants, and
{\mathfrak{C}}
for large universal constants, which
may be different from line by line. We use letters in mathcal mode, e.g.
{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{T}},{\mathcal{F}}
, to represent graphs, or subgraphs, and letters in mathbb mode, e.g.
\mathbb{T},\mathbb{X}
, to represent set of vertices.
For two quantities
X
and
Y
depending on
N
,
we write that
X=\OO(Y)
or
X\lesssim Y
if there exists some universal constant such
that
|X|\leqslant{\mathfrak{C}}Y
. We write
X=\oo(Y)
, or
X\ll Y
if the ratio
|X|/Y\rightarrow\infty
as
N
goes to infinity. We write

X\asymp Y
if there exists a universal constant
{\mathfrak{C}}>0
such that
Y/{\mathfrak{C}}\leqslant|X|\leqslant{\mathfrak{C}}Y
. We remark that the implicit constants may depend on
d
.
We denote
[\![{a,b}]\!]=[a,b]\cap\mathbb{Z}
and
[\![{N}]\!]=[\![{1,N}]\!]
. We say an event
\Omega
holds with high probability if
\mathbb{P}(\Omega)\geqslant 1-\oo(1)
; we say an event
\Omega
holds with overwhelming high probability, if for any
{\mathfrak{C}}>0
,

\mathbb{P}(\Omega)\geqslant 1-N^{-{\mathfrak{C}}}
holds provided
N
is large enough. For two random variables
X_{N},Y_{N}\geqslant 0
, we write
X_{N}\prec Y_{N}
to mean that for any small
\delta>0
and
N
large enough, with overwhelmingly high probability it holds
X_{N}\leqslant N^{\delta}Y_{N}
,

The research of J.H. is supported by NSF grant DMS-2331096 and the Sloan research award.
The research of T.M. is supported by NSF Grant DMS-2212881.
The research of H-T.Y. is supported by NSF grants DMS-1855509 and DMS-2153335.

2
Preliminaries

In this section we recall some results and concepts from
[
40
]
.
In
Section

2.1
we recall some useful estimates on the Green’s functions of random
d
-regular graphs. In
Section

2.2
, we recall local resampling and its properties.

2.1
Green’s Function Estimations

Here, and throughout the following, we use the notation

z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta

for the decomposition of
z
in the upper half complex plane, into its real and imaginary parts, and
\kappa=\kappa(z)=\min\{|E-2|,|E+2|\}
.

In the rest of this paper, we fix the degree
d\geqslant 3
, and arbitrarily small constants
0<{\mathfrak{c}}<{\mathfrak{a}}<1
. We also take a large radius
{\mathfrak{R}}:=({\mathfrak{c}}/4)\log_{d-1}N
, a length parameter
({\mathfrak{c}}/128)\log_{d-1}N\leqslant\ell\leqslant({\mathfrak{c}}/64)\log_{d-1}N
so that
N^{{\mathfrak{c}}/128}\leqslant(d-1)^{\ell}\leqslant N^{{\mathfrak{c}}/64}
. We recall the spectral domain from (
1.7
)

\mathbf{D}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\vcentcolon|E|\leqslant 2+{\mathfrak{a}},0<\eta\leqslant 1/{\mathfrak{a}},N\eta\sqrt{\min\{|E-2|,|E+2|\}+\eta}\geqslant N^{{\mathfrak{a}}}\}.

(2.1)

We recall the integer
\omega_{d}\geqslant 1
from
[
40
, Definition 2.6]
, which is the number of cycles a graph of degree at most
d
can have while its Green’s function has exponential decay. Rather than go through the technical definition, the important properties are that
\omega_{d}\geqslant 1
and that
\omega_{d}
is nondecreasing in
d
.

Definition 2.1
.

Fix
d\geqslant 3
and a sufficiently small
0<{\mathfrak{c}}<1
,
{\mathfrak{R}}=({\mathfrak{c}}/4)\log_{d-1}N
. We define the event
\overline{\Omega}
, where the following occur:

1.

The number of vertices that do not have a radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
tree neighborhood is at most
N^{{\mathfrak{c}}}
.

2.

The radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
neighborhoods of all vertices have excess at most
\omega_{d}
.

The event
\overline{\Omega}
is a typical event. The following proposition from
[
40
, Proposition 2.1]
states that
\overline{\Omega}
holds with high probability.

Proposition 2.2

(
[
40
, Proposition 2.1]
)
.

\overline{\Omega}
occurs with probability
1-\OO(N^{-(1-{\mathfrak{c}})\omega_{d}})
.

As we will see, once we are in
\overline{\Omega}
, Green’s functions can be approximated by tree extensions with overwhelmingly high probability. Our starting point is the bound from
[
40
, Theorem 4.2]
, which gives a polynomially small bound on the approximation of the Green’s functions. For this, we recall
Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})
defined in (
1.10
). We recall the idea of a Green’s function extension with general weight
\Delta(z)
from
[
40
, Section 2.3]
.

Definition 2.3
.

Fix degree
d\geqslant 3
, and a graph
{\mathcal{T}}
with degrees bounded by
d
. We define the function
P({\mathcal{T}},z,\Delta(z))
as follows. Define
H({\mathcal{T}})
to be the normalized adjacency matrix of
{\mathcal{T}}
, and
D({\mathcal{T}})
to be the diagonal matrix of degrees of
{\mathcal{T}}
. Then

\displaystyle P({\mathcal{T}},z,\Delta(z)):=\frac{1}{-z+H({\mathcal{T}})-\frac{\Delta(z)}{d-1}(d\mathbb{I}-D({\mathcal{T}}))}.

(2.2)

When
\Delta(z)=m_{\rm sc}(z)
, (
2.2
) is the Green’s function of the tree extension of
{\mathcal{T}}
, i.e. extending
{\mathcal{T}}
by attaching copies of infinite
(d-1)
-ary trees to
{\mathcal{T}}
to make each vertex degree
d
.
We recall from
[
40
, Proposition 2.12]
, if the graph
{\mathcal{T}}
has excess at most
\omega_{d}
, and
{\rm diam}({\mathcal{T}})|\Delta(z)-m_{\rm sc}(z)|\ll 1
, then for any
i,j\in{\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle|P_{ii}({\mathcal{T}},z,\Delta(z))|\asymp 1,\quad|P_{ij}({\mathcal{T}},z,\Delta(z))|\lesssim\left(\frac{|m_{\rm sc}(z)|}{\sqrt{d-1}}\right)^{\dist_{\mathcal{T}}(i,j)}.

(2.3)

Typically, we will take our graph
{\mathcal{T}}
to be the ball of
{\mathcal{G}}
with some fixed radius, thus for any
r\geqslant 1
, we define

{\mathcal{B}}_{r}(i,{\mathcal{G}})
to be the ball of radius
r
around vertex
i
in
{\mathcal{G}}
. We similarly define
{\mathcal{B}}_{r}(i,j,{\mathcal{G}})={\mathcal{B}}_{r}(i,{\mathcal{G}})\cup{\mathcal{B}}_{r}(j,{\mathcal{G}})
. The following theorem gives polynomial bounds for the Green’s functions and the Stieltjes transform of the empirical eigenvalue distribution.

Theorem 2.4

(
[
40
, Theorem 4.2]
)
.

Fix any sufficiently small
0<{\mathfrak{r}}<{\mathfrak{a}}<1
, and
r={\mathfrak{r}}\log_{d-1}N
. For any
z:=E+\mathrm{i}\eta
, we define
\kappa=\min\{|E-2|,|E+2|\}
, and the error parameters

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\prime}(z):=(\log N)^{100}\left(N^{-{\mathfrak{r}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m_{d}(z)]}{N\eta}}+\frac{1}{(N\eta)^{2/3}}\right),\quad\varepsilon(z):=\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}(z)}{\sqrt{\kappa(z)+\eta(z)+\varepsilon^{\prime}(z)}}.

(2.4)

For any
{\mathfrak{C}}\geqslant 1
and
N
large enough, with probability at least
1-\OO(N^{-{\mathfrak{C}}})
with respect to the uniform measure on
\overline{\Omega}
,

|G_{ij}(z)-P_{ij}({\mathcal{B}}_{r}(i,j,{\mathcal{G}}),z,m_{sc}(z))|,\quad|Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})-m_{sc}(z)|,\quad|m(z)-m_{d}(z)|\lesssim\varepsilon(z).

(2.5)

uniformly for every
i,j\in[\![{N}]\!]
, and any
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta
with
\eta\geqslant N^{-1+{\mathfrak{a}}}
. We denote the event that (
2.5
) holds as
\Omega(z)
.

For any integer
\ell\geqslant 1
, we define the functions
X_{\ell}(\Delta(z),z),Y_{\ell}(\Delta(z),z)
as

\displaystyle X_{\ell}(\Delta(z),z)=P_{oo}({\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{X}}),z,\Delta(z)),\quad Y_{\ell}(\Delta(z),z)=P_{oo}({\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{Y}}),z,\Delta(z)),

(2.6)

where
{\mathcal{X}}
is the infinite
d
-regular tree with root vertex
o
, and
{\mathcal{Y}}
is the infinite
(d-1)
-ary tree with root vertex
o
.
If the context is clear, we will simply write
X_{\ell}(\Delta(z),z)=X_{\ell}(\Delta(z))
and
Y_{\ell}(\Delta(z),z)=Y_{\ell}(\Delta(z))
.

Then
m_{sc}(z)
is a fixed point of the function
Y_{\ell}
, i.e.
Y_{\ell}(m_{sc}(z))=m_{\rm sc}(z)
. And
X_{\ell}(m_{\rm sc}(z))=m_{d}(z)
. If we take
\Delta(z)=Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})
as in (
1.10
), then
Y_{\ell}(Q(z;{\mathcal{G}}))
recovers
Y_{\ell}(Q)
as in (
1.11
). The following proposition states that if
\Delta(z)
is sufficiently close to
m_{sc}(z)
, then
Y_{\ell}(\Delta(z))
is close to
m_{sc}(z)
,
X_{\ell}(\Delta(z))
is close to
m_{d}(z)
.

Proposition 2.5

(
[
40
, Proposition 2.10]
)
.

Given a function
\Delta(z)
, if
\ell|\Delta(z)-m_{sc}(z)|\ll 1
, then the functions
X_{\ell}(\Delta(z)),Y_{\ell}(\Delta(z))

satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}X_{\ell}(\Delta(z))-m_{d}(z)=\frac{d}{d-1}(m_{d}(z))^{2}(m_{sc}(z))^{2\ell}(\Delta(z)-m_{sc}(z))+\OO\left(\ell|\Delta(z)-m_{sc}(z)|^{2}\right),\end{split}

(2.7)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}Y_{\ell}(\Delta(z))-m_{sc}(z)=(m_{sc}(z))^{2\ell+2}(\Delta(z)-m_{sc}(z))\\
&+(m_{sc}(z))^{2\ell+3}\left(\frac{1-(m_{sc}(z))^{2\ell+2}}{1-(m_{sc}(z))^{2}}\right)(\Delta(z)-m_{sc}(z))^{2}+\OO\left(\ell^{2}|\Delta(z)-m_{sc}(z)|^{3}\right).\end{split}

(2.8)

We will later use the Taylor expansion of
Y_{\ell}(\Delta(z))
around
m_{\rm sc}(z)
and utilize the fact that according to (
2.8
) the derivatives of
Y_{\ell}(\Delta(z))
satisfies,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Y_{\ell}^{\prime}(\Delta(z))=m_{sc}^{2\ell+2}+2m_{sc}^{2\ell+3}\left(\frac{1-m_{sc}^{2\ell+2}}{1-m_{sc}^{2}}\right)(\Delta-m_{sc})+O(\ell^{2}|\Delta-m_{sc}|^{2}),\\
&Y_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}(\Delta(z))=2m_{sc}^{2\ell+3}\left(\frac{1-m_{sc}^{2\ell+2}}{1-m_{sc}^{2}}\right)+O(\ell^{2}|\Delta-m_{sc}|).\end{split}

(2.9)

As a consequence of
Theorem

2.4
, for
{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega(z)
, there is some sufficiently large integer
{\mathfrak{b}}\geqslant 1
such that for any
\eta\geqslant N^{-1+{\mathfrak{a}}}
,

|Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})-Y_{\ell}(Q(z;{\mathcal{G}}))|^{\mathfrak{b}},\quad|Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})-m_{\rm sc}(z)|^{\mathfrak{b}}\leqslant\frac{1}{N^{2}},

(2.10)

where
Y_{\ell}
is as defined in (
2.6
).
Moreover, for any
i,j\in[\![{N}]\!]
, if
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/2}(i,j,{\mathcal{G}})
is a tree (does not contain any cycle), then

\displaystyle|G_{ij}(z)-P_{ij}(z)|\lesssim\varepsilon(z),\quad|G_{ij}(z)-P_{ij}(z)|^{{\mathfrak{b}}}\leqslant\frac{1}{N^{2}},

where
P_{ij}
is the Green’s function of infinite
d
-regular tree (the tree extension of
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/2}(i,j,{\mathcal{G}})
).

2.2
Local Resampling

In this section, we recall the local resampling and its properties. This gives us the framework to talk about resampling from the random regular graph distribution as a way to get an improvement in our estimates of the Green’s function.

For any graph
{\mathcal{G}}
, we denote the set of unoriented edges by
E({\mathcal{G}})
,
and the set of oriented edges by
\vec{E}({\mathcal{G}}):=\{(u,v),(v,u):\{u,v\}\in E({\mathcal{G}})\}
.
For a subset
\vec{S}\subset\vec{E}({\mathcal{G}})
, we denote by
S
the set of corresponding non-oriented edges.
For a subset
S\subset E({\mathcal{G}})
of edges we denote by
[S]\subset[\![{N}]\!]
the set of vertices incident to any edge in
S
.
Moreover, for a subset
\mathbb{V}\subset[\![{N}]\!]
of vertices, we define
E({\mathcal{G}})|_{\mathbb{V}}
to be the subgraph of
{\mathcal{G}}
induced by
\mathbb{V}
.

Definition 2.6
.

A (simple) switching is encoded by two oriented edges
\vec{S}=\{(v_{1},v_{2}),(v_{3},v_{4})\}\subset\vec{E}
.
We assume that the two edges are disjoint, i.e. that
|\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}|=4
.
Then the switching consists of
replacing the edges
\{v_{1},v_{2}\},\{v_{3},v_{4}\}
by the edges
\{v_{1},v_{4}\},\{v_{2},v_{3}\}
.
We denote the graph after the switching
\vec{S}
by
T_{\vec{S}}({\mathcal{G}})
,
and the new edges
\vec{S}^{\prime}=\{(v_{1},v_{4}),(v_{2},v_{3})\}
by

T(\vec{S})=\vec{S}^{\prime}
.

The local resampling involves a fixed center vertex, we now assume to be vertex
o
,
and a radius
\ell
.
Given a
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}
, we abbreviate
{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
(which may not be a tree) and its vertex set
\mathbb{T}
.
The edge boundary
\partial_{E}{\mathcal{T}}
of
{\mathcal{T}}
consists of the edges in
{\mathcal{G}}
with one vertex in
\mathbb{T}
and the other vertex in
[\![{N}]\!]\setminus\mathbb{T}
.
We enumerate the edges of
\partial_{E}{\mathcal{T}}
as
\partial_{E}{\mathcal{T}}=\{e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{\mu}\}
, where
e_{\alpha}=\{l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha}\}
with
l_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{T}
and
a_{\alpha}\in[\![{N}]\!]\setminus\mathbb{T}
. We orient the edges
e_{i}
by defining
\vec{e}_{i}=(l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha})
.
We notice that
\mu
and the edges
e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{\mu}
depend on
{\mathcal{G}}
. The edges
e_{\alpha}
are distinct, but
the vertices
a_{\alpha}
are not necessarily distinct and neither are the vertices
l_{\alpha}
. Our local resampling switches the edge boundary of
{\mathcal{T}}
with randomly chosen edges in
{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}

if the switching is admissible (see below), and leaves them in place otherwise.
To perform our local resampling, we choose
(b_{1},c_{1}),\dots,(b_{\mu},c_{\mu})
to be independent, uniformly chosen oriented edges from the graph
{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}
, i.e.,
the oriented edges of
{\mathcal{G}}
that are not incident to
\mathbb{T}
,
and define

\vec{S}_{\alpha}=\{\vec{e}_{\alpha},(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\},\qquad{\bf S}=(\vec{S}_{1},\vec{S}_{2},\dots,\vec{S}_{\mu}).

(2.11)

The sets
\bf S
will be called the
resampling data
for
{\mathcal{G}}
. We remark that repetitions are allowed in the resampling data
(b_{1},c_{1}),(b_{2},c_{2}),\cdots,(b_{\mu},c_{\mu})
.
We define an indicator that will be crucial to the definition of switch.

Definition 2.7
.

For
\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
,
we define the indicator functions

I_{\alpha}\equiv I_{\alpha}({\mathcal{G}},{\bf S})=1

1.

the subgraph
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/4}(\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\},{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})})
after adding the edge
\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha}\}
is a tree;

2.

and
\dist_{{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}}(\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\},\{a_{\beta},b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\})>{{\mathfrak{R}}/4}
for all
\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]\setminus\{\alpha\}
.

The indicator function
I_{\alpha}
imposes two conditions. The first one is a “tree” condition, which ensures that

a_{\alpha}
and
\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}
are far away from each other, and their neighborhoods are trees.
The second one imposes an “isolation” condition, which ensures that we only perform simple switching when the switching pair is far away from other switching pairs. In this way, we do not need to keep track of the interaction between different simple switchings.

We define the
admissible set

\displaystyle{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}:=\{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]:I_{\alpha}({\mathcal{G}},{\bf S})\}.

(2.12)

We say that the index
\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
is
switchable
if
\alpha\in{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}
. We denote the set
\mathbb{W}_{\bf S}=\{b_{\alpha}:\alpha\in{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}\}
. Let
\nu:=|{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}|
be the number of admissible switchings and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{\nu}

be an arbitrary enumeration of
{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}
.
Then we define the switched graph by

T_{\bf S}({\mathcal{G}}):=\left(T_{\vec{S}_{\alpha_{1}}}\circ\cdots\circ T_{\vec{S}_{\alpha_{\nu}}}\right)({\mathcal{G}}),

(2.13)

and the resampling data by

T({\bf S}):=(T_{1}(\vec{S_{1}}),\dots,T_{\mu}(\vec{S_{\mu}})),\quad T_{\alpha}(\vec{S}_{\alpha})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\begin{cases}T(\vec{S}_{\alpha})&(\alpha\in{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}),\\
\vec{S}_{\alpha}&(\alpha\not\in{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}).\end{cases}

(2.14)

To make the structure more clear, we introduce an enlarged probability space.
Equivalent to the definition above, the sets
\vec{S}_{\alpha}
as defined in (
2.11
) are uniformly distributed over

\displaystyle{\sf S}_{\alpha}({\mathcal{G}})=\{\vec{S}\subset\vec{E}:\vec{S}=\{\vec{e}_{\alpha},\vec{e}\},\text{$\vec{e}$ is not incident to ${\mathcal{T}}$}\},

i.e., the set of pairs of oriented edges in
\vec{E}
containing
\vec{e}_{\alpha}
and another oriented edge in
{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}
.
Therefore
{\bf S}=(\vec{S}_{1},\vec{S}_{2},\dots,\vec{S}_{\mu})
is uniformly distributed over the set

{\sf S}({\mathcal{G}})=\sf S_{1}({\mathcal{G}})\times\cdots\times\sf S_{\mu}({\mathcal{G}})
.

We introduce the following notations on the probability and expectation with respect to the randomness of the
{\bf S}\in\sf S({\mathcal{G}})
.

Definition 2.8
.

Given any
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}
, we
denote
\mathbb{P}_{\bf S}(\cdot)
the uniform probability measure on
{\sf S}({\mathcal{G}})
;
and
\mathbb{E}_{\bf S}[\cdot]
the expectation over the choice of
{\bf S}
according to
\mathbb{P}_{\bf S}
.

The following claim from
[
40
, Lemma 7.3]
states that this switch is invariant under the random regular graph distribution.

Lemma 2.9

(
[
40
, Lemma 7.3]
)
.

Fix
d\geqslant 3
. We recall the operator
T_{\bf S}
from (
2.13
). Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a random
d
-regular graph and
{\bf S}
uniformly distributed over
{\sf S}({\mathcal{G}})
, then the graph pair
({\mathcal{G}},T_{\bf S}({\mathcal{G}}))
forms an exchangeable pair:

\displaystyle({\mathcal{G}},T_{\bf S}({\mathcal{G}}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle law}}{{=}}(T_{\bf S}({\mathcal{G}}),{\mathcal{G}}).

In the following two lemmas we show that with high probability with respect to the randomness of
{\bf S}
, the randomly selected edges
(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})
are far away from each other, and have large tree neighborhood. In particular
T_{\bf S}{\mathcal{G}}\in\overline{\Omega}
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Fix
d\geqslant 3
, a
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}\in\overline{\Omega}
and a vertex set
\mathbb{F}\subset[\![{N}]\!]
containing vertex
o
, with
|\mathbb{F}|\leqslant N^{{\mathfrak{c}}/2}
. We consider the local resampling around vertex
o
, with resampling data
\{(l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha}),(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
. Then with probability at least
1-N^{-1+2{\mathfrak{c}}}
over the randomness of the resampling data
{\bf S}
, we have for any
\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
,
\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}\cup\mathbb{F},\{b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\})\geqslant 3{\mathfrak{R}}
; and
for any
v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]},{\mathcal{G}})
, the radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
neighborhood of
v
is a tree.

Lemma 2.11
.

Fix
d\geqslant 3
, a
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}\in\overline{\Omega}
. We denote the set of resampling data
{\sf F}({\mathcal{G}})\subset{\sf S}({\mathcal{G}})
such that for any
\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
,
\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha},o\},\{b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\})\geqslant 3{\mathfrak{R}}
, and
for any
v\in\{l_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})
is a tree. The for any
{\bf S}\in{\sf F}({\mathcal{G}})
, it holds that
then
\mu=d(d-1)^{\ell}
,
{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}=[\![{\mu}]\!]
, and
{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}=T_{\bf S}({\mathcal{G}})\in\overline{\Omega}
. Moreover,
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{R}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
is a tree for any
v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
.

Proof of
Lemma

2.10
.

We sequentially select
(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})
uniformly random from
{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}
. For any fixed
\alpha
, we consider all edges that would break the requirements of the lemma. For the first requirement, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{{\bf S}}(\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathbb{F}\cup_{1\leqslant\beta\leqslant\alpha-1}\{b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\},\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\})\leqslant 3{\mathfrak{R}})\lesssim N^{-1}(|\mathbb{F}|+2\mu)d(d-1)^{3{\mathfrak{R}}}\leqslant N^{-1+3{\mathfrak{c}}/2},

(2.15)

where
\mathbb{P}_{{\bf S}}(\cdot)
is the probability with respect to the randomness of
{\bf S}
as in
Definition

2.8
.
For the second requirement, we recall that
{\mathcal{G}}\in\overline{\Omega}
, in which all vertices except for
N^{\mathfrak{c}}
many have radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
tree neighborhood. Thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{{\bf S}}({\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{R}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})\text{ is not a tree for some }v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\},{\mathcal{G}}))\leqslant N^{-1}N^{\mathfrak{c}}d(d-1)^{\ell}\leqslant N^{-1+3{\mathfrak{c}}/2}.

(2.16)

The claim of
Lemma

2.10
follows from union bounding over all
\alpha
using (
2.15
) and (
2.16
).
∎

Proof of
Lemma

2.11
.

Under our assumption, the radius
{\mathfrak{R}}/2\geqslant\ell
neighborhood of
o
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/2}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
, is a truncated
d
-regular tree at level
{\mathfrak{R}}/2
. Thus
\mu=d(d-1)^{\ell}
. Moreover, the neighborhoods
{\mathcal{B}}_{3{\mathfrak{R}}/2}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
, and
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\},{\mathcal{G}})
for
\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
are disjoint. It follows that
\dist_{{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})}}(\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\},\{a_{\beta},b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\})>{{\mathfrak{R}}/4}
for all
\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
, and the subgraph
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}/4}(\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\},{\mathcal{G}}^{(\mathbb{T})})
after adding the edge
\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha}\}
is a tree for all
\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
. We conclude that
{\mathsf{W}}_{\bf S}=[\![{\mu}]\!]
.

Next we show that for any vertex
v\in[\![{N}]\!]
, the excess of
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
is no bigger than that of
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})
. Then it follows that
{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}\in\overline{\Omega}
.
If
\dist(v,\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]})\geqslant{\mathfrak{R}}
, then
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})={\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})
, and the statement follows.
Otherwise either
v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(\{a_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]},{\mathcal{G}})\subset{\mathcal{B}}_{3{\mathfrak{R}}/2}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
or
v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\},{\mathcal{G}})
for some
\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
. We will discuss the first case. The second case can be proven in the same way, so we omit.
If
v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
, we denote
\min_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(v,\{l_{\alpha}\})=r\leqslant{\mathfrak{R}}
. Then
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
is a subgraph of

{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})\cup_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}-r-1}(\{c_{\alpha},{\mathcal{G}})
after removing
\{(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
and adding
\{(l_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
. By our assumption,
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}-r-1}(c_{\alpha},{\mathcal{G}})
are disjoint trees. We conclude that
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
is a tree.
If
v\not\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
,
we denote
\min_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(v,a_{\alpha})=r\leqslant{\mathfrak{R}}
, then
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})
is a subgraph of

{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})\cup_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}-r-1}(b_{\alpha},{\mathcal{G}})

after removing
\{(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
and adding
\{(a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
.
Again by our assumption,
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}-r-1}(c_{\alpha},{\mathcal{G}})
are disjoint trees, we conclude the excess of
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
is at most that of
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})
.

∎

3
Main Results and Proof Outlines

In
Section

3.1
, we state our main results
Theorem

3.2
on the high moments estimate of the self-consistent equation.
The proof of
Theorem

3.2
follows an iteration scheme, with foundational concepts introduced in
Section

3.2
and the proof outlined in
Section

3.3
. Furthermore, the proofs of both
Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.2
, utilizing
Theorem

3.2
as an input, are detailed in
Section

3.4
.

3.1
Main Theorems

We will use some more flexible error terms, which we define in the following. We notice that they depend on the graph
{\mathcal{G}}
and thus are random quantities. For simplicity of notation, we simply write
Q(z)=Q(z;{\mathcal{G}})
.

Definition 3.1
.

For any
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta
in the upper half plane, we introduce the control parameter

\displaystyle\Phi=\Phi(z):=N^{\mathfrak{c}}\cdot\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m(z)]}{N\eta}.

For any
p\geqslant 1
, we define the error parameter

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{p}=\Psi_{p}(z)&:=\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega(z))\bigg[(d-1)^{-\ell/2}(|Q(z)-Y_{\ell}(Q(z))|+\Phi(z))^{2p}\\
&+\sum_{k=0}^{2p-1}(|Q(z)-Y_{\ell}(Q(z))|+\Phi(z))^{k}(\Phi(z)+N^{-1+2{\mathfrak{c}}})^{2p-k}\\
&+(|Q(z)-Y_{\ell}(Q(z))|+\Phi(z))^{2p-2}\Phi(z)\frac{|1-Y^{\prime}_{\ell}(Q(z))|}{N\eta}\\
&+(d-1)^{2\ell}|Q(z)-m_{\rm sc}(z)|^{2}(|Q(z)-Y_{\ell}(Q(z))|+\Phi(z))^{2p-1}\bigg].\end{split}

(3.1)

The following is our main result on the high moments estimate of the self-consistent equation.

Theorem 3.2
.

For any integer
d\geqslant 3
and sufficiently small
0<{\mathfrak{c}}<{\mathfrak{a}}<1
, the Green’s function of a random
d
-regular graph satisfies that, for any
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbf{D}
(recall from (
1.7
)) and integer
p\geqslant 1
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{E}[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega(z))|Q(z)-Y_{\ell}(Q(z),z)|^{2p}]\lesssim\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{p}(z)].\end{split}

(3.2)

Moreover, (
3.2
) holds with the left side replaced by

\mathbb{E}[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega(z))|m(z)-X_{\ell}(Q(z),z)|^{2p}]
.

An easy reformulation of
Theorem

3.2
gives a useful bound, as stated in the following corollary.

Corollary 3.3
.

Adopt the notations and assumptions of Theorem
3.2
, the following holds

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{E}[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega(z))|Q(z)-Y_{\ell}(Q(z),z)|^{2p}]\\
&\lesssim N^{4p{\mathfrak{c}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega(z))\left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m(z)]+\sqrt{(1-Y^{\prime}_{\ell}(Q(z)))\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m(z)]}}{N\eta}+|Q(z)-m_{\rm sc}(z)|^{2}\right)^{2p}\right].\end{split}

(3.3)

Moreover, (
3.2
) holds with the left side replaced by

\mathbb{E}[{\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega(z))}|m(z)-X_{\ell}(Q(z),z)|^{2p}]
.

Proof of
Corollary

3.3
.

We will only prove the statement for
|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|
. The statement for
|m-X_{\ell}(Q)|
can be proven by the same way. First we notice that
(|x|+|y|)^{q}\lesssim|x|^{q}+|y|^{q}
for fixed
q\geqslant 0
, thus we can rewrite (
3.2
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{E}[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{2p}]\lesssim(d-1)^{-\ell/2}\mathbb{E}[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{2p}]\\
&+\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{2p-2}\Phi\frac{|1-Y^{\prime}_{\ell}(Q)|}{N\eta}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)\Phi^{2p-1}\frac{|1-Y^{\prime}_{\ell}(Q)|}{N\eta}\right]\\
&+\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)\sum_{k=0}^{2p-1}|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{k}(\Phi+N^{-1+2{\mathfrak{c}}})^{2p-k}\right]+\mathbb{E}[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)(\Phi+N^{-1+2{\mathfrak{c}}})^{2p}]\\
&+(d-1)^{2\ell}\left(\mathbb{E}[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{2p-1}|Q-m_{\rm sc}|^{2}]+\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)\Phi^{2p-1}|Q-m_{\rm sc}|^{2}\right]\right).\end{split}

(3.4)

We recall Young’s inequality, for
1/q+1/r=1
,
a/q+b/r\geqslant a^{1/q}b^{1/r}
. Take a small
\lambda>0
, we can bound

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{2p-2}\Phi\frac{|1-Y^{\prime}_{\ell}(Q)|}{N\eta}\leqslant\lambda|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{2p}+\lambda^{1-p}\left(\Phi\frac{|1-Y^{\prime}_{\ell}(Q)|}{N\eta}\right)^{p},\\
&|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{k}(\Phi+N^{-1+2{\mathfrak{c}}})^{2p-k}\leqslant\lambda|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{2p}+\lambda^{1-2p/k}(\Phi+N^{-1+2{\mathfrak{c}}})^{2p}\\
&|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{2p-1}(d-1)^{2\ell}|Q-m_{\rm sc}|^{2}\leqslant\lambda|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{2p}+\lambda^{-1/(2p-1)}N^{2p{\mathfrak{c}}}|Q-m_{\rm sc}|^{4p}.\end{split}

(3.5)

We can then plug (
3.5
) back into (
3.4
), we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{E}[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{2p}]\lesssim\lambda\mathbb{E}[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{2p}]\\
&+N^{4p{\mathfrak{c}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)\left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m]+\sqrt{(1-Y^{\prime}_{\ell}(Q))\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m]}}{N\eta}+|Q-m_{\rm sc}|^{2}\right)^{2p}\right].\end{split}

For sufficiently small
\lambda>0
, the claim (
3.3
) follows from rearranging the above expression.

∎

Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.2
are consequences of
Corollary

3.3
, we postpone the proofs of
Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.2
to
Section

3.4
.

3.2
Switching Edges and the Forest

As explained in the introduction
Section

1.1.1
, our new strategy to prove
Theorem

3.2
is an iteration scheme. At each iteration, we conduct local resampling and express the Green’s function of the switched graph in terms of the original graph. Subsequently, we demonstrate that each non-negligible term incorporates at least one “diagonal factor”, denoted as
G_{c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}}^{(b_{\alpha})}
, where
(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})
represents an edge selected during local resampling. With this observation, we execute a local resampling around
c_{\alpha}
and repeat the process.

Because almost all neighborhoods in the graph have no cycles, with high probability, the edges involved in local resamplings have large tree neighborhoods, and are typically far from each other. Therefore, we think of the edges involved as a forest. In this section we formalize this iterative scheme using a sequence of forests, which encode all the edges involved in local resamplings.

Fix
\mu:=d(d-1)^{\ell}
, which is the number of boundary edges for a
d
-regular tree truncated at level
\ell
. We start from a graph
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}:=({\bf i}_{0}=\{o_{0},i_{0}\},E_{0}:=\{e=\{o_{0},i_{0}\}\})
, which consists of a single edge. Then we construct
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
from
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
, by

•

extending
e
to a
d
-regular tree truncated at level
\ell
,
T_{\ell}(o_{0})
(with
o_{0}
as the root vertex);

•

adding
\mu
boundary edges
\{e^{\prime}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
to
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(o_{0})
;

•

adding
\mu
new (directed) switching edges
\{e_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
;

•

and adding
\mu_{0}\leqslant 2p-1
special edges
\{\widehat{e}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu_{0}}]\!]}
.

Figure 1:
An example of the local resampling performed on the graph. We switch the green edges, on the boundary of the radius
\ell
neighborhood of a vertex
o
, with randomly chosen edges in the graph. This creates new, blue edges, and a new boundary.

Therefore

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{1}:={\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(o_{0})\bigcup\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{1}.

(3.6)

where
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{1}=\{e^{\prime}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
,

{\mathcal{M}}_{1}=\{e_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
and

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{1}=\{\widehat{e}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu_{0}}]\!]}
.

In general, given the forest
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}=({\bf i}_{t},E_{t})
, with edge sets
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{t},{\mathcal{M}}_{t},\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{t}
constructed in last step,
we construct
{\mathcal{F}}_{t+1}=({\bf i}_{t+1},E_{t+1})
by

•

picking one of the switching edges (from last step)
e=(i_{t},o_{t})\in{\mathcal{M}}_{t}
and extending
e
to a
d
-regular tree truncated at level
\ell
,
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(o_{t})
(with
o_{t}
as the root vertex);

•

adding
\mu
boundary edges
{\mathcal{M}}_{t+1}^{\prime}=\{e_{\alpha}^{\prime}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
to
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(o_{t})
;

•

adding another
\mu
new (directed) switching edges
{\mathcal{M}}_{t+1}=\{e_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]}
;

•

and adding
\mu_{t}
special edges
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{t+1}=\{\widehat{e}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu_{t}}]\!]}
such that the total number of special edges is bounded by
2p-1
.

Explicitly, the new forest
{\mathcal{F}}_{t+1}=({\bf i}_{t+1},E_{t+1})
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathcal{F}}_{t+1}={\mathcal{F}}_{t}\cup{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(o_{t})\bigcup\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{t+1}\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{t+1}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{t+1},\end{split}

(3.7)

where
{\bf i}_{t+1},E_{t+1}
are its vertex set and edge set, respectively.

The forest
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
encodes the local resamplings up to the
t
-th step. Along with this, we denote

1.

the set of switching edges
{\mathcal{K}}_{t}=\{(o_{0},i_{0})\}\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{t}\cup{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{t}
;

2.

the set of special edges
{\mathcal{V}}_{t}=\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{2}\cup\cdots\cup\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{t}
, which is of size at most
2p-1
;

3.

the
core of the forest
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
as
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}=(i_{0},o_{0})\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{2}\cdots\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{t}
. Each connected component of
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
contains either one core edge, or a special edge;

4.

the set of unused core edges

{\mathcal{C}}_{t}^{\circ}={\mathcal{C}}_{t}\setminus\{(i_{0},o_{0}),\cdots,(i_{t-1},o_{t-1})\}
.

For the most of the later analysis, we will focus on one step, and simplify the notations as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}_{t},\quad{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{K}}_{t},\quad{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{V}}_{t},\quad{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{t},\quad{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}={\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}_{t},\\
&{\mathcal{F}}^{+}={\mathcal{F}}_{t+1},\quad{\mathcal{K}}^{+}={\mathcal{K}}_{t+1},\quad{\mathcal{V}}^{+}={\mathcal{V}}_{t+1},\quad{\mathcal{C}}^{+}={\mathcal{C}}_{t+1},\quad({\mathcal{C}}^{\circ})^{+}={\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}_{t+1}.\end{split}

(3.8)

We now describe the event that local resamplings select edges that are far away from each other. Our analysis will consist of giving loose bounds when this is not the case, and then a far tighter bound when this does occur. The following indicator functions can be used to make sure that our new resampling data does not fall near the switching edges of any previous switch and are far away from each other.

Definition 3.4
.

We consider a forest
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
(as in (
3.8
)) with core edges
{\mathcal{C}}
and special edges
{\mathcal{V}}
, viewed as a subgraph of a
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
. We denote the indicator function
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=1
if vertices close to core edges have radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
tree neighborhoods, and core edges are distance
3{\mathfrak{R}}
away from each other. Explicitly,
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
is given by

\displaystyle I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}):=\prod_{(u,v)\in{\mathcal{F}}}A_{uv}\prod_{(b,c)\in{\mathcal{C}}}\left(\prod_{v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(c,{\mathcal{G}})}\bm{1}({\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})\text{ is a tree})\right)\prod_{(b,c)\neq(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bm{1}(\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(c,c^{\prime})\geqslant 3{\mathfrak{R}}).

The following lemma states that this occurs with high probability.

Lemma 3.5
.

Fix a
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}\in\overline{\Omega}
, and a forest
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
(as in (
3.8
)), viewed as a subgraph of a
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
. If
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=1
, then with probability at least
1-N^{-1+2{\mathfrak{c}}}
over the randomness of the resampling data
{\bf S}
, we have
{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}\in\overline{\Omega}
,
I({\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}})=1
and
I({\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}})=1
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}=T_{\bf S}{\mathcal{G}}
.

Proof.

Thanks to
Lemma

2.10
with
\mathbb{F}
the vertex set of
{\mathcal{F}}
, with probability
1-N^{-1+2{\mathfrak{c}}}
over the randomness of the resampling data
{\bf S}
, we have that for any
\alpha\neq\beta\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
,
\dist_{\mathcal{G}}(\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}\cup\mathbb{F},\{b_{\beta},c_{\beta}\})\geqslant 3{\mathfrak{R}}
; and
for any
v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(\{b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]},{\mathcal{G}})
, the radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
neighborhood of
v
is a tree. It follows that
I({\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}})=1
. It also follows from
Lemma

2.11
that
{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}\in\overline{\Omega}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{R}}(v,{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
is a tree for any
v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
. One can then check that
I({\mathcal{F}},{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})=1
. This finishes the proof of
Lemma

3.5
.

∎

From the construction above, each connected component of
{\mathcal{F}}
is either a single edge, or a radius
\ell+1
ball, and each connected component contains a core edge or a special edge. The following proposition states that the total number of embeddings where
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=1
is approximately the same as that of choosing each connected component independently.

Proposition 3.6
.

Given a forest
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
as in (
3.8
), and a
d
-regular graph
{\mathcal{G}}\in\overline{\Omega}
, we have

\sum_{{\bf i}}I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}\left(1+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N^{1-2{\mathfrak{c}}}}\right)\right),

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{{\mathcal{F}}}=(Nd)^{\theta_{1}({\mathcal{F}})}\left(Nd[(d-1)!]^{1+d+d(d-1)+\cdots+d(d-1)^{\ell-1}}\right)^{\theta_{2}({\mathcal{F}})}.\end{split}

(3.9)

Here
\theta_{1}({\mathcal{F}})
is the number of connected components in
{\mathcal{F}}
which is a single edge; and
\theta_{2}({\mathcal{F}})
is the number of connected components in
{\mathcal{F}}
which is a ball of radius
\ell+1
. We remark that
Z_{\mathcal{F}}
depends only on the forest
{\mathcal{F}}
but not
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Proof of Proposition
3.6
.

We can prove (
3.9
) by
induction on the number of connected components. If
{\mathcal{F}}
consists of a single edge
{\mathcal{F}}=e=(b,c)
which is a core edge, then

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf i}}I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=\sum_{b,c}A_{bc}\prod_{v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(c,{\mathcal{G}})}\bm{1}({\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(v,{\mathcal{G}})\text{ is a tree}))=Nd\left(1+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N^{1-{\mathfrak{c}}/2}}\right)\right),

(3.10)

where we used the definition of
\overline{\Omega}
from
Definition

2.1
. If
{\mathcal{F}}=e
is a speical edge,
then

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf i}}I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=\sum_{e=(u,v)}A_{uv}=Nd.

(3.11)

If
{\mathcal{F}}
consists of a radius
(\ell+1)
-ball, then we can also first sum over its core edge. The number of choices of this is the same as (
3.10
). Then we sum over the remaining vertices. Each interior vertex of the radius
(\ell+1)
-ball contributes a factor
(d-1)!
, since there are
(d-1)!
ways to embed its children vertices. We get

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf i}}I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=Nd[(d-1)!]^{1+d+d(d-1)+\cdots+d(d-1)^{\ell-1}}\left(1+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N^{1-{\mathfrak{c}}/2}}\right)\right).

(3.12)

If the statement holds for
{\mathcal{F}}
with
\theta
connected components, next we show it for
{\mathcal{F}}
with
\theta+1
connected components. We can first sum over the indices corresponding to a connected component, fixing the other indices. For the summation, we can again first sum over the core edge
e=(b,c)
or the special edge. The indicator function
I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})
requires that any vertex
v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(c,{\mathcal{G}})
has a radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
-tree neighborhood, and is distance at least
3{\mathfrak{R}}
away from other core edges. By the same argument as in
Lemma

3.5
, the number of choices is

\displaystyle Nd\left(1+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N^{1-3{\mathfrak{c}}/2}}\right)\right).

Then we sum over the remaining vertices in this connected component. If it is a single edge, we get a factor similarly to (
3.10
) and (
3.11
); if it is a radius
(\ell+1)
-ball, we get a factor similarly to (
3.12
). Next we can sum over the remaining
\theta
connected components of
{\mathcal{F}}
, which gives (
3.9
).

∎

3.3
Proof outline for
Theorem

3.2

In the process of proving Theorem
3.2
, we will have to prove bounds on a number of functions that depend on the forests
{\mathcal{F}}
which encodes local resamplings. To classify these functions, we give the following definition.

Definition 3.7
.

Fix a large integer
p\geqslant 1
. Consider forest
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
as defined in (
3.8
), with switching edges
{\mathcal{K}}
, unused core edges
{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
, and special edges
{\mathcal{V}}
. Denote
P=P({\mathcal{F}},z,m_{sc}(z))
, and we call a function
R_{\bf i}

generic with respect to

{\mathcal{F}}
if
R_{\bf i}=(G_{cc}^{(b)}-Y_{\ell}(Q))R_{\bf i}^{\prime}
for some
(b,c)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
,
where
R_{\bf i}^{\prime}
satisfies the following conditions,

1.

R_{\bf i}^{\prime}
contains an arbitrary number of factors of the form
(d-1)^{2\ell}(G_{ss^{\prime}}-P_{ss^{\prime}})
for
s,s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}
;
(d-1)^{2\ell}(G_{cc}^{(b)}-Q)
for
(b,c)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
;
(d-1)^{2\ell}G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})}
for
(b,c)\neq(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
;
(d-1)^{2\ell}G_{ss^{\prime}}
for
s\in{\mathcal{K}},s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}
;
G_{uv},G_{vv},1/G_{uu}
for
(u,v)\in{\mathcal{V}}
;
1-Y^{\prime}_{\ell}(Q),Q-Y_{\ell}(Q),(d-1)^{2\ell}(Q-m_{\rm sc})

and their complex conjugates.

2.

For each special edge
(u,v)\in{\mathcal{V}}
, there exist at least two terms of the form
(d-1)^{2\ell}G_{ss^{\prime}}
for
s\in{\mathcal{K}},s^{\prime}\in\{u,v\}
, or their complex conjugates. The number of
1-Y^{\prime}_{\ell}(Q)
factors and its complex conjugate equals the number of special edges.

3.

The total number of special edges and,
Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)
factors and its complex conjugate is
2p-1
.

We can regroup the generic terms, depending how many extra factors they have, as given in the following definition.

Definition 3.8
.

Fix a large integer
p\geqslant 1
and a forest
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
as defined in (
3.8
), and we denote
P=P({\mathcal{F}},z,m_{\rm sc})
. For any
r\geqslant 0
, we define
{\mathcal{E}}_{r}^{{\mathcal{F}}}
to be the collection of generic terms
R_{\bf i}
which contains exact
r
factors of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\{(d-1)^{2\ell}(G_{cc}^{(b)}-Q)\}_{(b,c)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}},\quad\{(d-1)^{2\ell}G_{cc^{\prime}}^{(bb^{\prime})}\}_{(b,c)\neq(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}},\\
&\{(d-1)^{2\ell}(G_{ss^{\prime}}-P_{ss^{\prime}})\}_{s,s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}},\quad(d-1)^{2\ell}(Q-m_{\rm sc}).\end{split}

(3.13)

Thanks to
Theorem

2.4
, these factors in (
3.13
) are quite small, bounded by
(d-1)^{2\ell}\varepsilon(z)
. In particular, for
z\in\bf D
(as defined in (
1.7
)),
(d-1)^{2\ell}\varepsilon(z)\leqslant N^{-{\mathfrak{c}}}
. Thus, for a generic term in
{\mathcal{E}}_{r}^{\mathcal{F}}
, these additional factors contribute to a factor
(d-1)^{2r\ell}\varepsilon^{r}\leqslant N^{-r{\mathfrak{c}}}
. Therefore, if we can demonstrate that after each local resampling,
r
increases and a term remains generic in the sense of Definition
3.7
, then this is a favorable outcome, as after a finite number of steps, this will imply that the term is negligible.

Figure 2:
Here we give an example of the growth of the tree
{\mathcal{F}}
to
{\mathcal{F}}^{+}
. We take the edge
(i,o)
, then extend it into a depth
\ell
tree
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(o)
. We then add
\mu
boundary edges of the form
(l_{\alpha},a_{\alpha})
at the boundary of the tree, and
\mu
new directed switching edges, denoted
e_{\alpha}:=(b_{\alpha},c_{\alpha})
for
\alpha\in[\![{\mu}]\!]
.

The following two propositions state that to compute the expectation of a generic term
R_{\bf i}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{r}^{{\mathcal{F}}}
, we can perform a local resampling around some unused switching edge
(i,o)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
. Then we arrive at a weighted sum of the expectation of generic terms
R_{{\bf i}^{+}}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{r^{\prime}}^{{\mathcal{F}}}
over some forests
{\mathcal{F}}^{+}=({\bf i}^{+},E^{+})
(as described in (
3.8
)). These new terms either have an extra factor
(d-1)^{-\ell/2}
, or
r^{\prime}\geqslant r+1
. Then after a finite number of steps this will mean that all the terms are negligible.

Proposition 3.9
.

Given a forest
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
as in (
3.8
) and a generic term
R_{\bf i}=(G_{oo}^{(i)}-Y_{\ell}(Q))R^{\prime}_{\bf i}
. We perform an local resampling around
(i,o)\in{\mathcal{F}}
with resampling data
\bf S
, and denote
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}=T_{\bf S}({\mathcal{G}})
. Then

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\frac{1}{Z_{\mathcal{F}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)R_{\bf i}({\mathcal{G}})\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{Z_{\mathcal{F}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})I({\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}},{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}\in\Omega)(\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}-Y_{\ell}(Q))R^{\prime}_{\bf i}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}})\right]+\OO(\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{p}]).\end{split}

(3.14)

Proposition 3.10
.

Given a forest
{\mathcal{F}}=({\bf i},E)
and a generic term
R_{\bf i}=(G_{oo}^{(i)}-Y_{\ell}(Q))R^{\prime}_{\bf i}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{r}^{\mathcal{F}}
. We construct
{\mathcal{F}}^{+}=({\bf i}^{+},E^{+})
(as in
Section

3.2
given by (
3.8
)) by performing an local resampling around
(i,o)\in{\mathcal{F}}
with resampling data
\bf S
, and denote
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}=T_{\bf S}({\mathcal{G}})
. Then up to an error of size
\OO(\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{p}])
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\mathcal{F}}}\sum_{{\bf i}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})I({\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}})\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}},{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}\in\Omega)(\widetilde{G}_{oo}^{(i)}-Y_{\ell}(Q))R^{\prime}_{\bf i}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}})\right]

(3.15)

can be rewritten as a weighted sum of terms in the following form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(d-1)^{\ell/2}}\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}^{+}}}\sum_{{\bf i}^{+}}\mathbb{E}[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)I({\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}})R_{{\bf i}^{+}}({\mathcal{G}})],\quad R_{{\bf i}^{+}}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{r}^{{\mathcal{F}}^{+}},

(3.16)

or

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{{\mathcal{F}}^{+}}}\sum_{{\bf i}^{+}}\mathbb{E}[\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)I({\mathcal{F}}^{+},{\mathcal{G}})R_{{\bf i}^{+}}({\mathcal{G}})],\quad R_{{\bf i}^{+}}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{r^{\prime}}^{{\mathcal{F}}^{+}},\quad r^{\prime}\geqslant r+1

(3.17)

where the sum of total weights is
\OO(\poly(\ell))
.
In this way, we either gain an extra factor
(d-1)^{-\ell/2}
, or
r^{\prime}\geqslant r+1
and we gain some extra factor in the form of (
3.13
).

Proof of
Theorem

3.2
.

We will only prove the bound for
\mathbb{E}[{\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)}|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{2p}]^{1/2p}
, the bound for
\mathbb{E}[{\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)}|m(z)-X_{\ell}(Q)|^{2p}]^{1/2p}
can be proven in the same way.
Denote
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}=({\bf i}_{0}=(i,o),E=\{(i,o)\})
, we introduce an indicator function
I({\mathcal{F}}_{0},{\mathcal{G}})=1
if any vertex
v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}(o,{\mathcal{G}})
has a radius
{\mathfrak{R}}
tree neighborhood in
{\mathcal{G}}
. Then we split

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{E}[(Q-Y_{\ell}(Q))^{p}\overline{(Q-Y_{\ell}(Q))^{p}}{\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)}]\\
&=\frac{1}{Nd}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{o,i}A_{oi}(G_{oo}^{(i)}-Y_{\ell}(Q))(Q-Y_{\ell}(Q))^{p-1}\overline{(Q-Y_{\ell}(Q))^{p}}{\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)}\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{{\bf i}_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[I({\mathcal{F}}_{0},{\mathcal{G}})(G_{oo}^{(i)}-Y_{\ell}(Q))(Q-Y_{\ell}(Q))^{p-1}\overline{(Q-Y_{\ell}(Q))^{p}}{\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)}\right]\\
&+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N^{1-2{\mathfrak{c}}}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[|Q-Y_{\ell}(Q)|^{2p-1}{\bm{1}({\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega)}\right],\end{split}

(3.18)

where we used that for
{\mathcal{G}}\in\Omega
,
A_{oi}|G_{oo}^{(i)}|,|Y_{\ell}(Q)|\lesssim 1
from
Theorem

2.4
, and
I({\mathcal{F}}_{0},{\mathcal{G}})=1
except for
\OO(d(d-1)^{\ell}N^{{\mathfrak{c}}})
vertices from
Definition

2.1
.

We denote

\displaystyle R_{{\bf i}_{0}}=(G_{oo}^{(i)}-Y_{\ell}(Q))(Q-Y_{\ell}(Q))^{p-1}\overline{(Q-Y_{\ell}(Q))^{p}},

which satisfies the conditions in
Definition

3.7
, thus
R_{{\bf i}_{0}}
is generic with respect to
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
. With this notation, we can rewrite (
3.18
) as
